    "aaa2481d-3fbb-477b-a70d-11d323d1ac5f": {
      "id": "aaa2481d-3fbb-477b-a70d-11d323d1ac5f",
      "message": {
        "id": "aaa2481d-3fbb-477b-a70d-11d323d1ac5f",
        "author": {
          "role": "user",
          "name": null,
          "metadata": {}
        },
        "create_time": 1724142417.696785,
        "update_time": null,
        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "現代社会において、作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフの重要度は今後どのようになっていきますか？"
          ]
        },
        "status": "finished_successfully",
        "end_turn": null,
        "weight": 1,
        "metadata": {
          "request_id": "8b61005c4e602692-NRT",
          "message_source": null,
          "timestamp_": "absolute",
          "message_type": null
        },
        "recipient": "all",
        "channel": null
      },
      "parent": "e0675c58-f753-4a68-b270-ed1f6e04bbf3",
      "children": [
        "3aaffed9-9427-47d6-8c6c-c0dacdc28c8e"
      ]
    },
    "3aaffed9-9427-47d6-8c6c-c0dacdc28c8e": {
      "id": "3aaffed9-9427-47d6-8c6c-c0dacdc28c8e",
      "message": {
        "id": "3aaffed9-9427-47d6-8c6c-c0dacdc28c8e",
        "author": {
          "role": "assistant",
          "name": null,
          "metadata": {}
        },
        "create_time": 1724142478.608088,
        "update_time": null,
        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "現代社会において、作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフの重要度は今後ますます高まっていくと考えられます。これにはいくつかの理由があります。\n\n### 1. **高齢化社会の進展**\n   - **高齢化によるニーズの増加**: 日本は急速に高齢化が進んでおり、高齢者人口が増加しています。これに伴い、加齢に伴う認知症、運動機能の低下、言語障害などに対応するため、リハビリテーションやメンタルケアの専門スタッフがますます重要な役割を担うことになります。特に、作業療法士や言語聴覚士は、介護予防や高齢者の生活の質（QOL）向上に欠かせない存在となります。\n\n### 2. **発達障害やメンタルヘルスへの関心の高まり**\n   - **発達障害への理解と支援の拡充**: 発達障害に関する認知が進むにつれ、早期発見と支援が求められています。児童発達支援や療育施設では、子どもの発達をサポートするための専門職の需要が高まるでしょう。言語聴覚士や作業療法士、臨床心理士は、発達障害のある子どもに対する個別療育や支援において重要な役割を果たします。\n   - **メンタルヘルス問題の拡大**: 現代社会では、ストレスや精神的な問題が増加しており、心のケアがますます重要視されています。特に、臨床心理士の役割は、職場や学校、地域社会でのメンタルヘルス支援においてますます需要が増えると予想されます。うつ病、不安障害、職場ストレスなどの対応において、臨床心理士の重要性は今後さらに増していくでしょう。\n\n### 3. **地域包括ケアシステムの推進**\n   - **地域での包括的支援の重要性**: 高齢者や障がい者が住み慣れた地域で自立した生活を送るためには、地域包括ケアシステムの充実が不可欠です。作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフが地域の医療機関や福祉施設と連携し、個別のニーズに応じた支援を提供することが求められます。これにより、地域全体で支える包括的なケア体制が構築されるでしょう。\n\n### 4. **医療技術の進展によるリハビリテーションの高度化**\n   - **リハビリテーション技術の進歩**: 医療技術の進展により、リハビリテーション分野も高度化しています。手術後のリハビリや、先進的なリハビリ機器の活用が進む中で、作業療法士や言語聴覚士が専門的な知識を持ち、これらの技術を駆使して患者の回復を支援することが重要です。特に、高度なリハビリ技術を用いた訓練の需要は今後も増加していくと予想されます。\n\n### 5. **子どもの発達支援の拡充**\n   - **児童発達支援への重要度の増加**: 子どもの発達に課題を抱えるケースが増加している中、早期の発見と支援が重要です。特に、保育園、幼稚園、学校などの教育現場や家庭において、専門スタッフが関わり、発達に関する支援や指導を行う必要があります。今後、児童発達支援事業の拡大に伴い、これらの専門スタッフの重要性はさらに増すでしょう。\n\n### 6. **予防医療と生活習慣病対策**\n   - **予防医療の強化**: 健康寿命を延ばすためには、予防医療や生活習慣病の予防が重要です。作業療法士は、運動指導や生活改善指導を通じて、患者の健康維持や生活習慣病の予防に貢献します。また、言語聴覚士や臨床心理士も、コミュニケーション能力や精神的な健康をサポートする役割が強化されるでしょう。\n\n### 7. **多職種連携の強化**\n   - **チーム医療の推進**: 作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士は、医師や看護師、理学療法士などの他職種と連携して、チーム医療を提供する重要なメンバーです。今後、医療現場では多職種が連携して患者を支援する体制がますます重要視されるため、これらの専門スタッフの役割もさらに大きくなるでしょう。\n\n### まとめ\n今後、作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフは、高齢化社会の進展、発達支援の重要性、メンタルヘルス問題の拡大、地域包括ケアの推進といった多方面でますます需要が高まると考えられます。これらの専門職は、地域社会や医療現場において、予防から治療、リハビリテーションまで広範な分野で活躍することが期待されており、現代社会における不可欠な存在となり続けるでしょう。"
          ]
        },

    "aaa20a02-1139-4d0f-a41a-003f72d4ba1f": {
      "id": "aaa20a02-1139-4d0f-a41a-003f72d4ba1f",
      "message": {
        "id": "aaa20a02-1139-4d0f-a41a-003f72d4ba1f",
        "author": {
          "role": "user",

        "create_time": 1724142543.239094,

        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "児童発達支援施設において、作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフの充実が図られた場合、不登校児等の問題などいろいろな社会的課題に対しても有効であると考えますが、実際はどうでしょうか？"
          ]
        },
        "status": "finished_successfully",
        "end_turn": null,
        "weight": 1,
        "metadata": {
          "request_id": "8b61036d4ffc8a90-NRT",
          "message_source": null,
          "timestamp_": "absolute",
          "message_type": null
        },
        "recipient": "all",
        "channel": null
      },
      "parent": "3aaffed9-9427-47d6-8c6c-c0dacdc28c8e",
      "children": [
        "b2674239-9270-4c5d-aae7-8ef17c4f0f37"
      ]
    },
    "b2674239-9270-4c5d-aae7-8ef17c4f0f37": {
      "id": "b2674239-9270-4c5d-aae7-8ef17c4f0f37",
      "message": {
        "id": "b2674239-9270-4c5d-aae7-8ef17c4f0f37",
        "author": {
          "role": "assistant",
          "name": null,
          "metadata": {}
        },
        "create_time": 1724142602.063959,
        "update_time": null,
        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "児童発達支援施設において作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフが充実することは、不登校児などの社会的課題に対しても有効な支援を提供できる可能性があります。以下の理由から、それが実際に効果的であると考えられます。\n\n### 1. **不登校児への心理的サポート**\n   - **臨床心理士の役割**: 不登校児の多くは、学校環境に適応するのが難しく、ストレスや不安、抑うつ感など精神的な問題を抱えていることが多いです。臨床心理士が支援することで、こうした心理的問題を早期に発見し、適切なカウンセリングや心理療法を提供することが可能です。不登校児の心のケアを行うことで、学校復帰のための心の準備が整い、再び学びの場に戻ることが促進されます。\n\n### 2. **社会的スキルの向上と再適応**\n   - **作業療法士による支援**: 不登校児の中には、社会的スキルや日常生活のスキルが不足している子どもが多くいます。作業療法士がこれらのスキルを強化するための訓練を提供することで、子どもが自信を持ち、学校生活や社会生活に適応しやすくなります。具体的には、集団活動や遊びを通じて、他者とのコミュニケーションスキルや協調性を育むことができます。\n   - **学校での再適応支援**: 作業療法士が、学習活動や日常生活活動の中で子どもの苦手な部分を見つけ、それに対して具体的な訓練を行うことで、子どもたちが再び学校生活に適応しやすくなります。\n\n### 3. **言語コミュニケーション能力の改善**\n   - **言語聴覚士による言語発達支援**: 言語やコミュニケーションに問題を抱える不登校児に対して、言語聴覚士が支援することにより、コミュニケーション能力が向上し、学校生活における対人関係の問題が緩和される可能性があります。これにより、学校での対話や集団活動に参加することへの不安が軽減されることが期待されます。\n\n### 4. **発達障害と不登校の関連性**\n   - **発達障害のサポート**: 不登校の原因として、発達障害が背景にある場合も多いです。たとえば、自閉スペクトラム症（ASD）や注意欠如・多動性障害（ADHD）の子どもたちは、学校環境に適応するのが難しいため、不登校になりやすい傾向があります。作業療法士や言語聴覚士、臨床心理士が早期に介入することで、発達障害の特性に応じた適切なサポートが提供され、子どもの発達支援が行き届くことで、不登校のリスクを低減することが可能です。\n\n### 5. **親と教師への支援**\n   - **家族支援と連携強化**: 専門スタッフが子どもだけでなく、親や教師に対するサポートも提供することで、学校と家庭の連携が強化されます。臨床心理士が親に対してカウンセリングを行い、子どもの状況を共有し、親の対応力を高めることで、不登校の要因を家庭環境からも改善することができます。また、教師に対しても、子どもの特性に応じた指導法をアドバイスすることで、学校での対応が柔軟になり、子どもが学校生活に再適応しやすくなります。\n\n### 6. **予防的なアプローチ**\n   - **早期発見と介入**: 専門スタッフが充実していることで、子どもの小さな変化や兆候を早期に察知し、適切なサポートが提供されます。これにより、問題が深刻化する前に不登校などの社会的課題に対応できるため、予防的なアプローチとしても非常に有効です。\n\n### 7. **長期的な支援の提供**\n   - **継続的なフォローアップ**: 児童発達支援施設では、子どもが必要とする支援を継続的に提供することができるため、短期的な改善だけでなく、長期的な支援計画が可能です。不登校の子どもに対しても、学校復帰後も継続的なサポートを行うことで、再び不登校になることを防ぐことができます。\n\n### 結論\n作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフが児童発達支援施設で充実することは、不登校児を含むさまざまな社会的課題に対して有効なアプローチとなります。これらの専門スタッフは、心理的支援、社会的スキルの向上、言語発達支援などを通じて、子どもが学校や社会に再適応しやすくなるようサポートします。また、早期発見と介入により、問題が深刻化する前に対処することで、予防的な効果も期待されます。"
          ]
        },

        "end_turn": true,

    "aaa22a8d-9308-4c60-911e-1317e6fe7e06": {
      "id": "aaa22a8d-9308-4c60-911e-1317e6fe7e06",
      "message": {
        "id": "aaa22a8d-9308-4c60-911e-1317e6fe7e06",

        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "児童発達支援施設において、作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフの充実が図られた場合の社会全体への影響はどうでしょうか？\n\n"
          ]
        },
        "status": "finished_successfully",
        "end_turn": null,
        "weight": 1,
        "metadata": {
          "request_id": "8b6104f8ff908a90-NRT",

      "parent": "b2674239-9270-4c5d-aae7-8ef17c4f0f37",

    "c09916b0-a83f-4b19-85d1-a878e5983456": {
      "id": "c09916b0-a83f-4b19-85d1-a878e5983456",
      "message": {
        "id": "c09916b0-a83f-4b19-85d1-a878e5983456",
        "author": {
          "role": "assistant",
          "name": null,
          "metadata": {}
        },
        "create_time": 1724142670.072381,
        "update_time": null,
        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "児童発達支援施設において、作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフが充実することは、社会全体に対して多大な影響を与えると考えられます。以下はその具体的な影響です。\n\n### 1. **子どもの健全な発達と将来的な社会参加の促進**\n   - **発達支援による早期介入効果**: 専門スタッフの充実によって、発達障害や発達遅延を抱える子どもに対して早期に適切な支援が行われることで、子どもたちの成長と発達が促進されます。これにより、子どもたちは将来的に自立した生活を送るための基礎を築くことができ、学校教育や職場への適応もスムーズに進むため、社会全体での社会参加が向上します。\n\n### 2. **メンタルヘルスの向上と予防的ケア**\n   - **心の健康のサポート**: 臨床心理士が心理的なサポートを提供することで、子どもたちのメンタルヘルスが向上し、精神的な問題が深刻化する前に対処できます。これにより、将来的な精神的疾患のリスクが低減され、社会全体での医療費や福祉コストの削減につながることが期待されます。また、子どもたちが心理的に安定することで、犯罪や暴力などの社会問題も予防される可能性があります。\n\n### 3. **教育現場への支援と効果的な学習環境の構築**\n   - **教育現場でのサポート強化**: 専門スタッフが児童発達支援施設だけでなく、教育現場とも連携することで、発達障害のある子どもが適切な教育支援を受けやすくなります。これにより、学校での学習効果が向上し、教育現場全体の質が高まるとともに、教師の負担軽減にもつながります。また、学校全体での教育環境がより包摂的で多様性を受け入れるものとなり、すべての子どもが学びやすい環境が整備されます。\n\n### 4. **社会的排除の防止と包摂社会の実現**\n   - **インクルーシブ社会の促進**: 専門スタッフが充実することで、障がいを持つ子どもや発達に遅れのある子どもが適切な支援を受けることが可能になります。これにより、社会的排除を防止し、すべての子どもが平等に社会に参加できるインクルーシブ社会が実現されます。障がいの有無にかかわらず、すべての子どもが自分の能力を最大限に発揮できる社会が形成されることで、社会全体が多様性を受け入れ、より強固で豊かなコミュニティが育まれます。\n\n### 5. **家族への支援と地域社会の安定化**\n   - **家庭の負担軽減とサポート**: 専門スタッフが充実することで、発達障害や発達遅延のある子どもを持つ家庭へのサポートが強化され、家庭全体の負担が軽減されます。これにより、家族全体の生活が安定し、経済的な負担も軽くなるため、社会的な安定が促進されます。また、家族のストレスが軽減されることで、地域社会における家族の役割も強化され、コミュニティの結束が高まります。\n\n### 6. **労働市場への長期的な影響**\n   - **労働力の質の向上**: 発達障害や発達遅延のある子どもたちが適切な支援を受け、将来的に社会に参加できるようになることで、労働市場に多様な人材が供給されます。これは、企業や組織が異なるバックグラウンドを持つ人材の力を活用し、イノベーションや生産性の向上に貢献することを意味します。また、専門職の充実により、介護や支援を必要とする高齢者や障がい者が自立できるようになることで、社会全体の労働力の質が向上します。\n\n### 7. **医療・福祉コストの削減**\n   - **予防的アプローチによるコスト削減**: 早期介入や支援が行き届くことにより、発達障害やメンタルヘルス問題の早期発見が可能になり、長期的な医療や福祉サービスの必要性が減少します。これにより、社会全体での医療費や福祉コストが削減され、他の分野に資源を投入する余裕が生まれます。\n\n### 8. **多職種連携による社会福祉の向上**\n   - **チームアプローチの促進**: 作業療法士、言語聴覚士、臨床心理士などの専門スタッフが充実することで、多職種が連携しながら包括的な支援を提供することが可能になります。これにより、地域福祉全体の質が向上し、すべての世代の福祉水準が向上します。多職種連携が進むことで、各分野の専門知識が融合し、より効果的なケアが提供されます。\n\n### まとめ\n児童発達支援施設において専門スタッフが充実することは、子どもたちの健全な成長を促進し、社会全体に対する積極的な影響を生み出します。心理的ケア、教育支援、社会的包摂の促進に加え、家族支援や医療コストの削減、労働市場への貢献といった広範な影響を及ぼし、より包括的で持続可能な社会を構築する基盤となります。このような取り組みは、社会全体の福祉向上と経済的な安定に寄与することが期待されます。"
          ]
        },

        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "児童発達に関する問題と地域内の公園施設の充実には密接な関係があります。公園施設は、子どもの身体的・社会的・精神的な発達を促進する場として重要な役割を果たしており、その充実は児童発達の支援に大きく貢献します。\n\n### 1. **身体発達への影響**\n   - **運動能力の向上**: 公園施設が充実していると、子どもたちは自由に体を動かす機会を得られます。ブランコや滑り台、ジャングルジムなどの遊具を通じて、平衡感覚や筋力、柔軟性などの身体機能が自然に発達します。運動が得意でない子どもでも、楽しい遊びを通じて体を動かすことができ、身体発達に良い影響を与えます。\n\n### 2. **社会性の発達への影響**\n   - **他者との交流機会の提供**: 公園は、子どもたちが同年代の友達や兄弟姉妹と一緒に遊び、他者との関係性を学ぶ場でもあります。公園施設が充実していることで、子どもたちは自然な形で他者と交流し、協力する力や順番を守ること、衝突を避けるコミュニケーション方法などを身につける機会が増えます。これにより、社会性や協調性が育まれます。\n   - **集団遊びの促進**: 公園での集団遊びは、ルールを理解し、他者と協力して目標を達成する力を養います。これらのスキルは、学校生活や社会生活でも必要となるものであり、児童発達における重要な要素です。\n\n### 3. **感覚統合の発達への貢献**\n   - **感覚統合療法の場としての利用**: 公園の遊具や自然環境は、感覚統合療法に有効です。たとえば、ブランコや滑り台は、バランス感覚や触覚、前庭感覚を刺激し、発達に課題のある子どもたちが楽しみながら感覚統合を促進することができます。公園が療育の場として活用されることで、専門家の支援がさらに効果的になることもあります。\n\n### 4. **精神的な健康への影響**\n   - **ストレス解消と心の安定**: 公園での遊びや自然との触れ合いは、子どもたちのストレスを軽減し、精神的な安定をもたらします。特に発達障害や不安を抱える子どもたちにとって、自然環境での活動はリラックス効果があり、メンタルヘルスに良い影響を与えます。外で遊ぶことで、感情のコントロールが促進され、心理的なバランスが整うことが期待されます。\n\n### 5. **親子の関わりの促進**\n   - **親子の交流機会の提供**: 公園は、親子が一緒に過ごす貴重な場となります。親子で一緒に遊んだり、外出したりすることによって、親子関係が深まり、子どもが安心感や信頼感を感じやすくなります。これにより、親子間でのコミュニケーションが増え、子どもの発達に対する理解や支援も自然と深まることがあります。\n\n### 6. **地域コミュニティの形成**\n   - **地域の子育てネットワークの構築**: 公園は、親同士の交流の場ともなり、地域内の子育てネットワークを形成する重要な場となります。子育てに関する情報共有や悩み相談が行われることで、地域全体が子どもの成長を支える体制が整います。このようなコミュニティの充実は、児童発達に間接的に寄与します。\n\n### 7. **公園の設備のバリアフリー化**\n   - **障がい児も楽しめる施設の整備**: 公園施設がバリアフリー化され、障がいのある子どもたちも安心して遊べる環境が整えば、すべての子どもたちが平等に発達を促進できる場が提供されます。例えば、車椅子でも使用可能な遊具や、安全性に配慮したデザインの遊具があることで、障がいを持つ子どもたちも社会参加の機会を得られます。\n\n### まとめ\n地域内の公園施設が充実することで、子どもの身体的、社会的、感覚的、精神的な発達が促進されるため、児童発達支援に大きな貢献を果たします。さらに、公園が親子の交流の場となり、地域コミュニティを強化することも、子どもの健全な成長に寄与します。このように、公園施設の充実は、地域全体で子どもたちの発達を支援するための重要な要素となり得ます。"
          ]
        },

    "aaa2879e-0c65-47af-acf4-f6946e434490": {
      "id": "aaa2879e-0c65-47af-acf4-f6946e434490",
      "message": {
        "id": "aaa2879e-0c65-47af-acf4-f6946e434490",
        "author": {
          "role": "user",

        "create_time": 1724143007.126629,
        "update_time": null,
        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "上記関係性は科学的な根拠は証明されているのでしょうか？"
          ]
        },
        "status": "finished_successfully",
        "end_turn": null,
        "weight": 1,
        "metadata": {
          "request_id": "8b610ec04aec25ef-NRT",
          "message_source": null,
          "timestamp_": "absolute",
          "message_type": null
        },
        "recipient": "all",
        "channel": null
      },
      "parent": "c76fdea3-0f9a-4de6-94bc-fccf7bb76fb8",
      "children": [
        "0b9357db-cd7b-4814-8434-fcb1f6f442d4"
      ]
    },
    "0b9357db-cd7b-4814-8434-fcb1f6f442d4": {
      "id": "0b9357db-cd7b-4814-8434-fcb1f6f442d4",
      "message": {
        "id": "0b9357db-cd7b-4814-8434-fcb1f6f442d4",
        "author": {
          "role": "assistant",
          "name": null,
          "metadata": {}
        },
        "create_time": 1724143059.350827,
        "update_time": null,
        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "地域内の公園施設の充実と児童発達との関係について、科学的な根拠は多数の研究や調査によって裏付けられています。以下に、科学的根拠を示すいくつかの主要な研究分野とその成果を紹介します。\n\n### 1. **身体活動と発達の関連性**\n   - **身体発達に関する研究**: 公園などの屋外活動が子どもの運動能力や身体発達に与える影響について、複数の研究が行われています。例えば、米国小児科学会（AAP）は、子どもたちが定期的に身体を動かすことが、筋力、心肺機能、骨密度の向上につながり、全体的な健康に寄与することを報告しています。また、屋外での自由な遊びが、バランス感覚やコーディネーション（協調運動能力）を発達させることも確認されています。\n   - **プレイグラウンドの設計と身体発達**: 公園の設備やデザインが多様であるほど、子どもたちの運動スキルが多角的に発展するという研究もあります。特に、複雑な遊具がある公園は、子どもたちがさまざまな身体運動を経験する機会を提供し、発達に有益とされています。\n\n### 2. **社会性と集団遊びの効果**\n   - **社会的スキルの発達**: 公園での集団遊びが、子どもの社会的スキルの発達に寄与するという証拠も多く存在します。例えば、遊びの中での対話や協力、ルールの理解といった行動が、コミュニケーション能力や社会的適応力を育てるとされています。公園は、こうしたスキルを発展させるための重要な場として、研究者からも注目されています。\n   - **社会的相互作用の促進**: 公園は、異なる家庭環境や文化的背景を持つ子どもたちが自然に出会い、相互に交流する場でもあります。こうした異文化交流が子どもの柔軟性や共感能力を育てることも、多くの研究で示されています。\n\n### 3. **感覚統合と屋外環境の影響**\n   - **感覚統合療法の研究**: 感覚統合に関する研究では、屋外活動が感覚処理能力に与える影響が確認されています。特に、自然環境や公園での遊びが、感覚過敏や感覚統合の問題を持つ子どもたちに有効であることが示されています。ブランコや滑り台といった遊具を使用した活動が、バランス感覚や触覚の刺激を通じて感覚統合を促進することが、科学的に証明されています。\n\n### 4. **精神的健康と自然環境**\n   - **メンタルヘルスに関する研究**: 自然環境での活動が、ストレスの軽減や心理的安定に寄与するという研究結果も数多く報告されています。自然との触れ合いが、子どもたちの感情の調整能力を高め、不安やストレスを軽減することが確認されています。さらに、屋外活動が自己肯定感や注意力の向上に役立つことも、研究で明らかにされています。\n   - **「注意回復理論」**: 自然環境が精神的な疲労を回復させ、集中力や注意力を改善する「注意回復理論（Attention Restoration Theory）」が提唱されており、公園での活動がこの理論に基づいてメンタルヘルスを改善するという科学的根拠が示されています。\n\n### 5. **親子関係と地域コミュニティ**\n   - **親子の絆の強化に関する研究**: 公園での親子の共同活動が、親子間の絆を強化し、子どもの情緒的な安定に寄与するという調査結果があります。親が子どもと遊びを通じて積極的に関わることが、子どもの自尊心や情緒的発達をサポートすることが科学的に証明されています。\n   - **地域コミュニティの強化**: 公園での活動が、地域住民同士の交流を促進し、地域コミュニティの強化につながることも研究で示されています。コミュニティの結束が強まることで、子どもの健全な発達を支える環境が整い、地域全体でのサポートが強化されます。\n\n### 6. **公園のバリアフリー化とインクルーシブ性**\n   - **インクルーシブデザインの研究**: バリアフリー化された公園施設が、障がい児を含むすべての子どもに平等な発達の機会を提供することが研究で示されています。すべての子どもがアクセスできる公園は、社会的包摂（インクルージョン）の促進に寄与し、障がい児の発達支援においても重要な役割を果たしています。\n\n### まとめ\n児童発達と公園施設の充実に関する科学的根拠は多数の研究によって証明されています。公園での遊びや自然環境への接触は、子どもの身体的・社会的・感覚的・精神的な発達を促進し、全体的な健全な成長を支える重要な要素です。さらに、公園は地域社会の結束を高め、親子関係を強化し、すべての子どもが平等に発展できるインクルーシブな環境を提供する場としても非常に重要です。"
          ]
        },

    "e033e595-3447-4d56-b638-fdec4cbeb6df": {
      "id": "e033e595-3447-4d56-b638-fdec4cbeb6df",
      "message": {
        "id": "e033e595-3447-4d56-b638-fdec4cbeb6df",

        "create_time": 1724143117.591543,

        "content": {
          "content_type": "tether_quote",
          "url": "https://ijbnpa.biomedcentral.com/articles/10.1186/s12966-021-01203-x",
          "domain": "ijbnpa.biomedcentral.com",
          "text": "\nParticipants completed survey items, including age, sex, school year, dog ownership, number of days they were physically active for at least 60 min in in a typical week [【58†40】, 【59†41】], and time taken to walk from home to the park they visited most often. They also responded to items relating to their usual park visitation over the past 3 months: frequency and duration of park visitation; activities engaged in and activity levels when visiting parks; accompaniment at the park; frequency of meeting or talking to people they already knew, and to people they did not know, when at the park; and mode of transport used to travel to the park (see Table 【60†1】 for response options). These items have been used in previous studies with children of this age [【55†38】].\n\nTable 1 Demographic characteristics of participants (n = 252)\n\n【61†Full size table】\n\nIn a previous phase of the ProjectPARK study [【55†38】], children (n = 274) rated images of 42 park features according to how likely it is that the features would encourage them to engage in park-based physical activity. The top ten rated features from that study (based on mean scores) were identified separately for both boys and girls equating to 11 features, as the features in the top ten varied by sex. Previous research among adolescents in Belgium showed that preference for park features can vary according to frequency of park visitation and engagement in physical activity [【62†42】], thus, we also calculated mean scores and rankings for each feature according to frequency of park visitation (i.e. visit ≥ once/week, visit < once/week) and whether or not participants met physical activity recommendations (i.e., ≥ 60 mins MVPA/day, < 60 mins MVPA/day) via self-reported days per week they performed at least 60 min MVPA/day. Two additional features were in the top ten when ratings were examined by these two sub-groups; large grassy open space was ranked 10th for those who reported visiting parks <once/week, and sports walls were ranked 8th for those who did not meet physical activity recommendations (data not shown). This resulted in the inclusion of written descriptions for a total of 13 features (see Table 【63†2】). Printed handouts showing images of examples of these features were also available to students whilst they were completing the survey. Each feature was presented with two levels - present or absent (e.g., sports goals, no sports goals); or three or four levels - high-to-low sequence order (e.g., giant slides, medium slides, no slides).\n\nTable 2 Park features and feature levels\n\n【64†Full size table】\n\nA series of ACBC tasks were completed by all participants to identify preferences for features and feature levels for encouraging park-based physical activity. An ACBC survey is interactive as tasks are customised in real time based on individual preferences reported as participants progress through the survey [【65†43】]. In the previous rating study features were examined independently or in isolation, whereas with ACBC the features are examined conjointly. ACBC surveys tend to be realistic of people’s real life decision-making as they employ non-compensatory procedures [【66†44】]. Participants were required to complete each task before progressing to the next task. Firstly, respondents selected six features from the list of 13 that would be most likely to encourage them to be active in the park. If any of the six selected features had three or four levels, participants were required to select their most preferred level. For example, for the feature ‘slides’, participants were asked to select their preferred level: ‘giant slides’, ‘medium slides’, or ‘no slides’.\n\nRespondents were then shown a series of six ‘screening’ questions where four parks were described; each park had a different combination of feature levels for the six selected features. For each park, participants were required to indicate if the park described ‘would’ or ‘would not’ encourage them to be active. Based on responses to these screening questions, the program identified if any particular feature levels were included in parks that were selected as ‘not encouraging’ them to be active. In those cases, participants were asked additional question(s) to indicate whether any of the identified feature levels would be ‘totally unacceptable’ and to select the one feature level that was most unacceptable. Similarly, when the program’s algorithm detected that certain feature levels were consistently included, respondents were asked to select the feature level that a park ‘must have’ to encourage them to be active when at the park. These ‘must have’ and ‘unacceptable’ questions determined whether specific feature levels were non-compensatory for choice and ensured that remaining tasks included features levels that best met each individual’s needs.\n\nParticipants were then shown a series of 13 ‘choice tasks’ that included descriptions of two parks. They were asked to select the park that was most appealing for encouraging them to be active. Each park had a different combination of the levels of the six features that had been identified in the previous sections as ‘possibilities’ (i.e., feature levels had not been marked as ‘unacceptable’). The “winning” park profile for each choice task tournament was included in the following rounds of the choice task tournament until the most preferred profile was determined [【66†44】]. Examples of each of the main tasks are included in Additional file 1. Pilot testing was performed with two girls and two boys aged 7-12 years, and minor amendments for clarity were made accordingly.\n\n### Data analyses \n\nDescriptive characteristics of the sample were calculated using Stata version 15 (Stata Corp., College Station, TX, USA). Data from the ACBC survey were analysed using Sawtooth SSI Web Lighthouse Studio 9.8.0. ACBC analysis yield two t",

    "94050042-5dcb-4c51-88f1-77ccf6412b55": {
      "id": "94050042-5dcb-4c51-88f1-77ccf6412b55",
      "message": {
        "id": "94050042-5dcb-4c51-88f1-77ccf6412b55",

        "content": {

          "url": "https://jeas.springeropen.com/articles/10.1186/s44147-022-00152-x",
          "domain": "jeas.springeropen.com",
          "text": "\n### Playgrounds and children’s physical activity \n\nThe availability of a variety of play areas and equipment that allow both structured and unstructured activities, such as climbing, swinging, and games such as tag, and “make believe” activities, adds quality to parks [【61†44】]. Floyd et al. [【27†10】] reported that within parks, the physical activity levels of girls are generally lower than those of boys. However, according to Korca et al. [【62†45】], playgrounds are the only activity setting in parks that attracts both boys and girls equally. Smith [【63†46】] also suggested that boys are more willing to take risks than girls during play, particularly in playgrounds. Previous research shows that the type of playground and the equipment provided influence children’s creativity and physical activity levels [【64†47】, 【65†48】]. According to the literature [【66†49】,【67†50】,【68†51】], playgrounds can be classified into three main types: traditional playgrounds (categorized by the presence of unconnected play equipment such as slides, swings, seesaws, and climbers), contemporary playgrounds (in which the different pieces of play equipment are typically connected together to form multi-purpose continuous structures with multiple entry and exit points and that are usually aesthetically pleasing), and adventure playgrounds (which incorporate various types of loose materials—old tires, large building blocks, ropes, logs and various pieces of wood, buckets and water containers, sand and sand toys, and a variety of natural materials—that allow children to create their own environment or play equipment). Outdoor playgrounds are intended to enhance children’s physical, social, emotional, and cognitive development through play and are typically designed to provide a relatively safe playing environment [【69†52】]. However, several researchers have argued for the developmental benefits of the less predictable and more dynamic play spaces for children over those designed for safety [【70†53】, 【71†54】]. According to Withagen et al. [【72†55】], one of the factors determining the attractiveness of a playground to children is the effort needed to actualize an affordance in that playground, as it has been suggested that children are attracted to challenging opportunities that imply risky behavior [【51†34】].\n\n## Methods\n\n### Research design \n\nAs previously mentioned, the objective of the study is to investigate, in the Egyptian context, the design characteristics of the green areas and playgrounds of urban parks that promote different levels of children’s physical activity. To achieve this objective, the study investigated six settings—three green areas and three playgrounds—in a large urban park east of Cairo, namely “Family Park”. Family Park is a relatively new urban park in New Cairo with a total area of about 283,000 sqm. This park was selected for the study because it features several green areas and several playgrounds. In addition, the different green areas and the different playgrounds differ in terms of design characteristics. The study relied on a mixed qualitative-quantitative approach and several methods of data collection, including the field documentation of the design characteristics of the six settings investigated, structured observations, behavioral mapping, and semi-structured interviews with 16 children. The purpose of the structured observations was to quantitatively assess levels of children’s physical activity in the different settings investigated. Behavioral mapping was used to document the different activities children undertake in relation to the design features of the six settings. Finally, the purpose of the semi-structured interviews was to investigate children’s perceptions, opinions, and preferences about the different settings studied.\n\nBased on the literature discussing the age range for fundamental motor skills development, the target age group for this study was 5- to 11-year-old children [【36†19】,【37†20】,【38†21】]. Children under the age of 5 were not included because it is only from the age of five that children can interpret their experiences and feelings in relation to their environments and can express their preferences about the places they use and the activities they undertake in those places [【73†56】]. Moreover, children under the age of five are often unable to use the playground on their own and are usually accompanied by a parent or guardian. Accordingly, only 5- to 11-year-old children were interviewed. For observations and behavior mapping, the determination of children’s age had to rely on judgment and only children who appeared within the targeted age group were considered.\n\n### The settings selected for the study \n\nSix settings within family park were selected for the study, three green areas and three playgrounds. Family park features several relatively well spatially defined green areas and a total of four playgrounds. Some of the green areas border a large artificial lake at the center of the park. The park also features a petting zoo, an outdoor theater, a small amusement park, and several buildings housing a museum, workshop spaces for children, indoor games, and restaurants. A main circular pathway connects the different areas and facilities of the park together.\n\nThe three green areas selected for the study—GA1, GA2, and GA3 (see Fig. 【74†1】)—were determined during pilot visits to be the green areas most used by children. These three green areas were also selected because, as detailed in Table 【75†1】, they differ in terms of physical characteristics such as area, topography, and types and diversity of vegetation. They also differ in their relation to the park’s central lake. GA1 overlooks the lake. GA2 surrounds the southern end of the lake. Howev",
          "title": "Urban park design and children’s physical activity levels: an investigation of design characteristics of green areas and playgrounds | Journal of Engineering and Applied Science | Full Text",
          "tether_id": null
        },

A main circular pathway connects the different areas and facilities of the park together.\n\nThe three green areas selected for the study—GA1, GA2, and GA3 (see Fig. 【74†1】)—were determined during pilot visits to be the green areas most used by children. These three green areas were also selected because, as detailed in Table 【75†1】, they differ in terms of physical characteristics such as area, topography, and types and diversity of vegetation.         "content": {
          "content_type": "tether_quote",
          "url": "https://www.mdpi.com/2071-1050/15/13/10073",
          "domain": "www.mdpi.com",
          "text": "\nclear\n\nfirst_page\n\n【68† Download PDF 】 \n\nsettings\n\n【69† Order Article Reprints 】 \n\nFont Type: \n\nArial Georgia Verdana\n\nFont Size:\n\nAa Aa Aa\n\nLine Spacing:\n\n  \n\nColumn Width:\n\n  \n\nBackground:\n\nOpen Access Article\n\n#  The Impact of a Child-Friendly Design on Children’s Activities in Urban Community Pocket Parks \n\nby \n\nLe Zhang\n\n[Image 0]Le Zhang\n\n【70† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【71† Scilit †scilit.net】【72† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【58† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,2, \n\nXiaoxiao Xu\n\n[Image 1]Xiaoxiao Xu\n\n【73† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【74† Scilit †scilit.net】【75† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【59† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,2 and \n\nYanlong Guo\n\n[Image 2]Yanlong Guo\n\n【76† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【77† Scilit †scilit.net】【78† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【60† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n3,*\n\n^{1}\n\nSchool of Arts, Anhui Jianzhu University, Hefei 230601, China\n\n^{2}\n\nAnhui Provincial Institute of Culture and Tourism Innovation and Development, Hefei 203106, China\n\n^{3}\n\nSocial Innovation Design Research Centre, Anhui University, Hefei 203106, China\n\n*\n\nAuthor to whom correspondence should be addressed. \n\nSustainability 2023, 15(13), 10073; 【79†https://doi.org/10.3390/su151310073†doi.org】 \n\nSubmission received: 11 May 2023 / Revised: 12 June 2023 / Accepted: 23 June 2023 / Published: 25 June 2023\n\n(This article belongs to the Special Issue 【80†Maintaining a Healthy Lifestyle: Sustainability of Promoting Physical Activity】)  \n\nDownload keyboard_arrow_down\n\n 【68†Download PDF】   \nDownload PDF with Cover   \nDownload XML   \n【81†Download Epub】   \n\nBrowse Figures \n\n 【82† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【83† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【84† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【85† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【86† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 \n\n【87†Review Reports】 【88†Versions Notes】 \n\n## Abstract\n\n:\n\nUrbanization is a global trend that is expected to continue, and by 2025, it is estimated that almost 60% of the world’s children will live in urban areas. Urban community pocket parks provide a solution to the need for parks in high-density urban communities due to their flexible location, small size, and patchy distribution. This paper aims to examine and optimize the construction of urban community pocket parks from a child’s perspective to encourage children’s participation in these parks. The first step was to conduct a literature review to identify key evaluation indicators for assessing the child-friendliness of pocket parks. Then, the AHP-entropy TOPSIS approach was used to establish an indicator system to effectively evaluate the child-friendliness of pocket parks in urban communities. The system included physical space, cognitive ability, emotional development, environmental perception, and social interaction. Finally, suggestions for optimization were made based on the weighting of influencing factors. The results show that freedom of movement (6.2%) significantly affects the child-friendliness ratings of community pocket parks. Additionally, Hefei residents are not sufficiently influenced by the diversity of play (2.29%) and play facility planning (2.58%) in pocket parks. Therefore, consideration should be given to focusing on the degree of nature adaptation in park construction and renewal projects, as well as understanding children’s perception of nature.\n\nKeywords: \n\n【89†child-friendly environment】; 【90†pocket parks】; 【91†evaluation framework】; 【92†AHP】; 【93†entropy weights】; 【94†TOPSIS】\n\n##  1. Introduction \n\nIn June 2019, the China Community Development Association launched the “Code for Building Child-Friendly Communities in China”. According to this code, a neighborhood is defined as a social community of people residing within a certain geographical area [【95†1】]. As globalization continues to increase and cities undergo modernization, urban population growth has surged significantly. The United Nations projects that by 2050, 68% of the global population will be concentrated in urban centers. With this pace of urbanization, it is expected that, by 2025, urban areas will be home to around 60% of the world’s children. However, there is a significant tension between children and cities due to the growing size of cities and the increasing demands for quality of life. Therefore, promoting child-friendly cities is essential for providing good quality of life and accessibility for all children in urban areas. This is especially important in public spaces such as pocket parks, where children are most active. Child-friendly design in such spaces can create a safe, enjoyable, and stimulating environment that promotes sustainable physical activity for children [【96†2】].\n\nA pocket park is defined as a park that is smaller than most urban parks [【97†3】], and the definition of pocket park size varies from region to region, from less than half an acre to less than 5000 square meters [【98†4】]. American scholar Robert Zane designed the world’s first pocket park in 1963, marking the birth of the micropark out of which the prototype was a small park scattered in high-density urban cen",
          "title": "Sustainability | Free Full-Text | The Impact of a Child-Friendly Design on Children&rsquo;s Activities in Urban Community Pocket Parks",
          "tether_id": null
        },

                "text": "\nclear\n\nfirst_page\n\n【68† Download PDF 】 \n\nsettings\n\n【69† Order Article Reprints 】 \n\nFont Type: \n\nArial Georgia Verdana\n\nFont Size:\n\nAa Aa Aa\n\nLine Spacing:\n\n  \n\nColumn Width:\n\n  \n\nBackground:\n\nOpen Access Article\n\n#  The Impact of a Child-Friendly Design on Children’s Activities in Urban Community Pocket Parks \n\nby \n\nLe Zhang\n\n[Image 0]Le Zhang\n\n【70† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【71† Scilit †scilit.net】【72† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【58† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,2, \n\nXiaoxiao Xu\n\n[Image 1]Xiaoxiao Xu\n\n【73† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【74† Scilit †scilit.net】【75† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【59† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,2 and \n\nYanlong Guo\n\n[Image 2]Yanlong Guo\n\n【76† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【77† Scilit †scilit.net】【78† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【60† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n3,*\n\n^{1}\n\nSchool of Arts, Anhui Jianzhu University, Hefei 230601, China\n\n^{2}\n\nAnhui Provincial Institute of Culture and Tourism Innovation and Development, Hefei 203106, China\n\n^{3}\n\nSocial Innovation Design Research Centre, Anhui University, Hefei 203106, China\n\n*\n\nAuthor to whom correspondence should be addressed. \n\nSustainability 2023, 15(13), 10073; 【79†https://doi.org/10.3390/su151310073†doi.org】 \n\nSubmission received: 11 May 2023 / Revised: 12 June 2023 / Accepted: 23 June 2023 / Published: 25 June 2023\n\n(This article belongs to the Special Issue 【80†Maintaining a Healthy Lifestyle: Sustainability of Promoting Physical Activity】)  \n\nDownload keyboard_arrow_down\n\n 【68†Download PDF】   \nDownload PDF with Cover   \nDownload XML   \n【81†Download Epub】   \n\nBrowse Figures \n\n 【82† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【83† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【84† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【85† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【86† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 \n\n【87†Review Reports】 【88†Versions Notes】 \n\n## Abstract\n\n:\n\nUrbanization is a global trend that is expected to continue, and by 2025, it is estimated that almost 60% of the world’s children will live in urban areas. Urban community pocket parks provide a solution to the need for parks in high-density urban communities due to their flexible location, small size, and patchy distribution. This paper aims to examine and optimize the construction of urban community pocket parks from a child’s perspective to encourage children’s participation in these parks. The first step was to conduct a literature review to identify key evaluation indicators for assessing the child-friendliness of pocket parks. Then, the AHP-entropy TOPSIS approach was used to establish an indicator system to effectively evaluate the child-friendliness of pocket parks in urban communities. The system included physical space, cognitive ability, emotional development, environmental perception, and social interaction. Finally, suggestions for optimization were made based on the weighting of influencing factors. The results show that freedom of movement (6.2%) significantly affects the child-friendliness ratings of community pocket parks. Additionally, Hefei residents are not sufficiently influenced by the diversity of play (2.29%) and play facility planning (2.58%) in pocket parks. Therefore, consideration should be given to focusing on the degree of nature adaptation in park construction and renewal projects, as well as understanding children’s perception of nature.\n\nKeywords: \n\n【89†child-friendly environment】; 【90†pocket parks】; 【91†evaluation framework】; 【92†AHP】; 【93†entropy weights】; 【94†TOPSIS】\n\n##  1. Introduction \n\nIn June 2019, the China Community Development Association launched the “Code for Building Child-Friendly Communities in China”. According to this code, a neighborhood is defined as a social community of people residing within a certain geographical area [【95†1】]. As globalization continues to increase and cities undergo modernization, urban population growth has surged significantly. The United Nations projects that by 2050, 68% of the global population will be concentrated in urban centers. With this pace of urbanization, it is expected that, by 2025, urban areas will be home to around 60% of the world’s children. However, there is a significant tension between children and cities due to the growing size of cities and the increasing demands for quality of life. Therefore, promoting child-friendly cities is essential for providing good quality of life and accessibility for all children in urban areas. This is especially important in public spaces such as pocket parks, where children are most active. Child-friendly design in such spaces can create a safe, enjoyable, and stimulating environment that promotes sustainable physical activity for children [【96†2】].\n\nA pocket park is defined as a park that is smaller than most urban parks [【97†3】], and the definition of pocket park size varies from region to region, from less than half an acre to less than 5000 square meters [【98†4】]. American scholar Robert Zane designed the world’s first pocket park in 1963, marking the birth of the micropark out of which the prototype was a small park scattered in high-density urban cen",

          "text": "\nArial Georgia Verdana\n\nFont Size:\n\nAa Aa Aa\n\nLine Spacing:\n\n  \n\nColumn Width:\n\n  \n\nBackground:\n\nOpen Access Review\n\n#  Determining Factors in the Use of Urban Parks That Influence the Practice of Physical Activity in Children: A Systematic Review \n\nby \n\nRosario Padial-Ruz\n\n[Image 0]Rosario Padial-Ruz\n\n【73† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【74† Scilit †scilit.net】【75† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【59† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1, \n\nMª Esther Puga-González\n\n[Image 1]Mª Esther Puga-González\n\n【76† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【77† Scilit †scilit.net】【78† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【60† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,*, \n\nÁlvaro Céspedes-Jiménez\n\n[Image 2]Álvaro Céspedes-Jiménez\n\n【79† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【80† Scilit †scilit.net】【81† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【61† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n2 and \n\nDavid Cabello-Manrique\n\n[Image 3]David Cabello-Manrique\n\n【82† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【83† Scilit †scilit.net】【84† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【62† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n2\n\n^{1}\n\nDepartment of Didactics of Musical, Plastic and Corporal, University of Granada, 18011 Granada, Spain\n\n^{2}\n\nDepartment of Physical Education and Sports, University of Granada, 18011 Granada, Spain\n\n*\n\nAuthor to whom correspondence should be addressed. \n\nInt. J. Environ. Res. Public Health 2021, 18(7), 3648; 【85†https://doi.org/10.3390/ijerph18073648†doi.org】 \n\nSubmission received: 22 February 2021 / Revised: 19 March 2021 / Accepted: 24 March 2021 / Published: 31 March 2021\n\n(This article belongs to the Special Issue 【86†Physical Activity in Childhood and Adolescence】)  \n\nDownload keyboard_arrow_down\n\n 【71†Download PDF】   \nDownload PDF with Cover   \nDownload XML   \n【87†Download Epub】   \n\nBrowse Figure \n\n 【88† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 \n\n【89†Versions Notes】 \n\n## Abstract\n\n:\n\nThe design and/or remodelling of urban parks is a good health strategy to alleviate the lack of physical activity (PA) in children and, consequently, the different health problems derived from this. The main objective of the present study was to obtain a systematic review of the design features and characteristics that influence users’ visits to urban parks and the PA engagement in them. A literature search was carried out in the Web of Science (WOS) and Scopus databases during the months of June and July 2020. After considering and applying inclusion criteria, the final review sample was formed of 31 scientific papers published between 2010–2020. The results obtained in the review lead us to conclude that the needs of the population (children and family members who care for them) and socio-economic context of the area in which they are built must be considered when constructing and/or remodelling parks. Involving community members in playground renovations can have a positive effect on park use and PA engagement in children.\n\nKeywords: \n\n【90†urban parks】; 【91†children】; 【92†user perception】; 【93†design】; 【94†physical activity】; 【95†health】\n\n##  1. Introduction \n\nThe high rates of inactivity and sedentariness amongst children worldwide is a major problem that needs to be addressed immediately [【96†1】]. World Health Organisation (WHO) recommendations for physical activity (PA) in young people, aged 5–17 years, state at least 60 min per day of moderate to vigorous intensity activity. This activity should be mostly aerobic and should incorporate vigorous activities that particularly strengthen muscles and bones at least three times per week. However, over the past few years, we have seen an increase in the proportion of the population that does not comply with these recommendations. This includes a large number of children [【97†2】,【98†3】], with more children also coming from disadvantaged settings [【99†4】].\n\nInactivity at early ages can lead to high obesity rates. Excess body fat in the early stages of life can have serious health consequences at later stages, since the risk of contracting diseases derived from inactivity, such as diabetes, hypertension and coronary diseases, among others, is high and dangerous [【100†5】]. Thus, according to Omorou et al. [【101†6】], lack of PA may increase the likelihood of being obese in adulthood.\n\nAnother consequence of childhood obesity comes from the association between motor skills, PA and body composition in the pre-school stage. Diminished interaction with natural environments and the restriction of movement at increasingly younger ages can lead to poor execution of the motor tasks necessary for correct psychomotor development [【102†7】].\n\nAt a motor level, children will also present greater difficulties at a later stage when doing sports. Children with higher physical activity levels and less sedentary behaviour present better motor skills compared with those who mostly engage in sedentary pursuits, with the latter often unable to develop such skills adequately [【103†8】]. Nonetheless, inactivity not only causes problems at a physical and physiological level, but it also plays a fundamental role with regards to",

    "fb6cd121-9b14-459b-a21f-0594283e6d3b": {
      "id": "fb6cd121-9b14-459b-a21f-0594283e6d3b",
      "message": {
        "id": "fb6cd121-9b14-459b-a21f-0594283e6d3b",
        "author": {

        "content": {
          "content_type": "tether_quote",
          "url": "https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10389-017-0828-x",
          "domain": "link.springer.com",
          "text": "\nA negative binomial model was a better fit for the data than a Poisson model with overdispersion or a zero-inflated Poisson model assuming independence of all observations. The independence assumption clearly holds for observations from different playgrounds and may be justified for observations on different days in the same playground. However, some observations (scans) were done within an hour in the same playground. Consequently, all observations carried out on the same day in the same playground are correlated and form a cluster. In total, we had 194 clusters consisting of 1–9 observations. Therefore, we opted for a generalized estimating equation (GEE) approach using negative binomial distribution. For the working correlation structure, we used the compound symmetry (exchangeable) correlation structure because of the relatively short total observation periods. Statistical significance was considered at p < 0.05.\n\n## Results \n\nA total of 578 scans were conducted, and all playgrounds were visited for observation 18–21 times. Of the ten study playgrounds, one had a neighborhood-level catchment area, six a quarter-level catchment area and three a district-level catchment area. The size of the playgrounds ranged from 280 m² to 3800 m². The descriptive statistics on playground features are provided in Table 【45†1】. The number of playground users across all scans ranged from 0 to 43 (Table 【46†2】). In total, 2538 children (1284 girls and 1254 boys) were observed in the playgrounds. The mean number of children in any given playground was 4.39 (SD = 5.11) and ranged between 0.94 and 10.08. The mean number of active children was 2.24 (SD = 2.82) and ranged between 0.12 and 4.91. On average, about the same number of girls and boys played in the playgrounds (2.22 ± 2.82 and 2.17 ± 2.70, respectively). The mean number of children being at least moderately physically active was 1.03 (SD = 1.58) girls and 1.21 (SD = 1.68) boys. On average, 2.10 (SD = 2.97) children in the playgrounds were sedentary.\n\nTable 1 Descriptive statistics of playground features (N = 10)\n\n【47†Full size table】\n\nTable 2 Number of children using playgrounds and number of children per physical activity level (MVPA, sedentary)\n\n【48†Full size table】\n\nStatistical results on the relationship between playground features and usage and physical activity levels in children and girls and boys, respectively, are presented in Tables 【49†3】, 【50†4】 and 【51†5】. Overall, the numbers of children present in playgrounds and the numbers of children being at least moderately active were higher in playgrounds providing more varied play facilities (B = 0.54, 95% CI: 0.23–0.84; p < 0.001; B = 0.53, 95% CI: 0.19–0.86; p = 0.001) and in playgrounds without naturalness (B = −0.91, 95% CI: −1.55–−0.28; p = 0.005; B = −0.74, 95% CI: −1.46–−0.02; p = 0.045). None of the other variables predicted playground usage or physical activity levels in children in a multivariate model when disregarding gender. In girls (Table 【50†4】), play facility provision and naturalness predicted playground usage (B = 0.46, 95% CI: 0.12–0.80; p = 0.008; B = −0.74, 95% CI: −1.46–−0.03; p = 0.043), and play facility provision also predicted the number of girls being at least moderately physically active (B = 0.43, 95% CI: 0.05–0.81; p = 0.028). Furthermore, in playgrounds with no provision of multi-purpose areas, girls were more likely to be moderately physically active than in playgrounds with multi-purpose areas (B = −2.61, 95% CI: −4.89–0.33; p = 0.025). For boys, the number of users was higher in playgrounds providing more varied play facilities and without naturalness than in natural playgrounds with fewer types of play facilities. The results did not crucially differ in models without adjustment for rainfall and temperature.\n\nTable 3 Generalized estimating equation (GEE) for predicting playground usage and number of children being physically active in playgrounds (MVPA)\n\n【52†Full size table】\n\nTable 4 Generalized estimating equation (GEE) for predicting playground usage in girls and number of girls being physically active in playgrounds (MVPA)\n\n【53†Full size table】\n\nTable 5 Generalized estimating equation (GEE) for predicting playground usage in boys and number of boys being physically active in playgrounds (MVPA)\n\n【54†Full size table】\n\n## Discussion \n\nThe current research project aimed to investigate the relationship between playground features and usage and the physical activity levels of children in playgrounds, also taking gender differences into consideration. We were able to show that the provision of a variety of play facilities fostered playground usage in children and that playgrounds without naturalness were visited more frequently by children than natural playgrounds. This is one of ",

                "text": "\nA negative binomial model was a better fit for the data than a Poisson model with overdispersion or a zero-inflated Poisson model assuming independence of all observations. The independence assumption clearly holds for observations from different playgrounds and may be justified for observations on different days in the same playground. However, some observations (scans) were done within an hour in the same playground. Consequently, all observations carried out on the same day in the same playground are correlated and form a cluster. In total, we had 194 clusters consisting of 1–9 observations. Therefore, we opted for a generalized estimating equation (GEE) approach using negative binomial distribution. For the working correlation structure, we used the compound symmetry (exchangeable) correlation structure because of the relatively short total observation periods. Statistical significance was considered at p < 0.05.\n\n## Results \n\nA total of 578 scans were conducted, and all playgrounds were visited for observation 18–21 times. Of the ten study playgrounds, one had a neighborhood-level catchment area, six a quarter-level catchment area and three a district-level catchment area. The size of the playgrounds ranged from 280 m² to 3800 m². The descriptive statistics on playground features are provided in Table 【45†1】. The number of playground users across all scans ranged from 0 to 43 (Table 【46†2】). In total, 2538 children (1284 girls and 1254 boys) were observed in the playgrounds. The mean number of children in any given playground was 4.39 (SD = 5.11) and ranged between 0.94 and 10.08. The mean number of active children was 2.24 (SD = 2.82) and ranged between 0.12 and 4.91. On average, about the same number of girls and boys played in the playgrounds (2.22 ± 2.82 and 2.17 ± 2.70, respectively). The mean number of children being at least moderately physically active was 1.03 (SD = 1.58) girls and 1.21 (SD = 1.68) boys. On average, 2.10 (SD = 2.97) children in the playgrounds were sedentary.\n\nTable 1 Descriptive statistics of playground features (N = 10)\n\n【47†Full size table】\n\nTable 2 Number of children using playgrounds and number of children per physical activity level (MVPA, sedentary)\n\n【48†Full size table】\n\nStatistical results on the relationship between playground features and usage and physical activity levels in children and girls and boys, respectively, are presented in Tables 【49†3】, 【50†4】 and 【51†5】. Overall, the numbers of children present in playgrounds and the numbers of children being at least moderately active were higher in playgrounds providing more varied play facilities (B = 0.54, 95% CI: 0.23–0.84; p < 0.001; B = 0.53, 95% CI: 0.19–0.86; p = 0.001) and in playgrounds without naturalness (B = −0.91, 95% CI: −1.55–−0.28; p = 0.005; B = −0.74, 95% CI: −1.46–−0.02; p = 0.045). None of the other variables predicted playground usage or physical activity levels in children in a multivariate model when disregarding gender. In girls (Table 【50†4】), play facility provision and naturalness predicted playground usage (B = 0.46, 95% CI: 0.12–0.80; p = 0.008; B = −0.74, 95% CI: −1.46–−0.03; p = 0.043), and play facility provision also predicted the number of girls being at least moderately physically active (B = 0.43, 95% CI: 0.05–0.81; p = 0.028). Furthermore, in playgrounds with no provision of multi-purpose areas, girls were more likely to be moderately physically active than in playgrounds with multi-purpose areas (B = −2.61, 95% CI: −4.89–0.33; p = 0.025). For boys, the number of users was higher in playgrounds providing more varied play facilities and without naturalness than in natural playgrounds with fewer types of play facilities. The results did not crucially differ in models without adjustment for rainfall and temperature.\n\nTable 3 Generalized estimating equation (GEE) for predicting playground usage and number of children being physically active in playgrounds (MVPA)\n\n【52†Full size table】\n\nTable 4 Generalized estimating equation (GEE) for predicting playground usage in girls and number of girls being physically active in playgrounds (MVPA)\n\n【53†Full size table】\n\nTable 5 Generalized estimating equation (GEE) for predicting playground usage in boys and number of boys being physically active in playgrounds (MVPA)\n\n【54†Full size table】\n\n## Discussion \n\nThe current research project aimed to investigate the relationship between playground features and usage and the physical activity levels of children in playgrounds, also taking gender differences into consideration. We were able to show that the provision of a variety of play facilities fostered playground usage in children and that playgrounds without naturalness were visited more frequently by children than natural playgrounds. This is one of ",

    "f8274ebc-8f54-4501-abf6-94da9fce093e": {
      "id": "f8274ebc-8f54-4501-abf6-94da9fce093e",
      "message": {
        "id": "f8274ebc-8f54-4501-abf6-94da9fce093e",

          "role": "assistant",

        "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "以下の科学論文は、公園施設の充実と児童発達に関する科学的根拠を示す代表的な論文です。\n\n1. **\"Understanding children’s preference for park features that encourage physical activity: an adaptive choice based conjoint analysis\"**\n   - この研究では、子どもたちが公園で体を動かすために好む特徴を分析しています。公園の特定の特徴が子どもたちの身体活動にどのように影響を与えるかを示しており、身体活動を促進するためのデザインが発達における重要な役割を果たすことが示されています【162†source】。\n\n2. **\"Urban park design and children’s physical activity levels: an investigation of design characteristics of green areas and playgrounds\"**\n   - この論文は、公園内の遊具やデザインが子どもの身体活動に与える影響を調査しています。特に、さまざまな遊びの機会を提供する遊具が子どもの創造性や運動レベルに与える影響が詳述されており、児童発達における重要性が確認されています【163†source】。\n\n3. **\"The Impact of a Child-Friendly Design on Children’s Activities in Urban Community Pocket Parks\"**\n   - この研究では、子どもにやさしいデザインが都市のコミュニティポケットパークでの子どもたちの活動にどのように影響を与えるかを調査しています。公園のデザインが自由な動きや環境認識、感情発達に与える影響が強調されており、発達の促進におけるデザインの重要性が示されています【164†source】。\n\n4. **\"Determining Factors in the Use of Urban Parks That Influence the Practice of Physical Activity in Children: A Systematic Review\"**\n   - この体系的レビューは、都市公園の使用と子どもの身体活動に影響を与える要因を調査しています。公園の設計やリモデリングが子どもの身体活動と健康に与える影響が取り上げられており、これが発達に与える影響について科学的に検証しています【165†source】。\n\n5. **\"Playground usage and physical activity levels of children based on playground spatial features\"**\n   - この研究では、遊び場の空間的な特徴と子どもの身体活動レベルとの関係が調査されています。遊具の多様性や自然環境の存在が、子どもの遊びや運動レベルにどのように影響するかが詳細に分析されており、児童発達における空間設計の役割が明らかにされています【166†source】。\n\nこれらの論文は、公園施設の設計と児童発達との関係性を科学的に証明しており、遊具や緑地のデザインが子どもの身体的・社会的発達にどのように影響を与えるかを明らかにしています。"
          ]
        },
        "status": "finished_successfully",
        "end_turn": true,
        "weight": 1,

                "type": "webpage",
                "title": "Understanding children’s preference for park features that encourage physical activity: an adaptive choice based conjoint analysis | International Journal of Behavioral Nutrition and Physical Activity | Full Text",
                "url": "https://ijbnpa.biomedcentral.com/articles/10.1186/s12966-021-01203-x",
                "text": "\nParticipants completed survey items, including age, sex, school year, dog ownership, number of days they were physically active for at least 60 min in in a typical week [【58†40】, 【59†41】], and time taken to walk from home to the park they visited most often. They also responded to items relating to their usual park visitation over the past 3 months: frequency and duration of park visitation; activities engaged in and activity levels when visiting parks; accompaniment at the park; frequency of meeting or talking to people they already knew, and to people they did not know, when at the park; and mode of transport used to travel to the park (see Table 【60†1】 for response options). These items have been used in previous studies with children of this age [【55†38】].\n\nTable 1 Demographic characteristics of participants (n = 252)\n\n【61†Full size table】\n\nIn a previous phase of the ProjectPARK study [【55†38】], children (n = 274) rated images of 42 park features according to how likely it is that the features would encourage them to engage in park-based physical activity. The top ten rated features from that study (based on mean scores) were identified separately for both boys and girls equating to 11 features, as the features in the top ten varied by sex. Previous research among adolescents in Belgium showed that preference for park features can vary according to frequency of park visitation and engagement in physical activity [【62†42】], thus, we also calculated mean scores and rankings for each feature according to frequency of park visitation (i.e. visit ≥ once/week, visit < once/week) and whether or not participants met physical activity recommendations (i.e., ≥ 60 mins MVPA/day, < 60 mins MVPA/day) via self-reported days per week they performed at least 60 min MVPA/day. Two additional features were in the top ten when ratings were examined by these two sub-groups; large grassy open space was ranked 10th for those who reported visiting parks <once/week, and sports walls were ranked 8th for those who did not meet physical activity recommendations (data not shown). This resulted in the inclusion of written descriptions for a total of 13 features (see Table 【63†2】). Printed handouts showing images of examples of these features were also available to students whilst they were completing the survey. Each feature was presented with two levels - present or absent (e.g., sports goals, no sports goals); or three or four levels - high-to-low sequence order (e.g., giant slides, medium slides, no slides).\n\nTable 2 Park features and feature levels\n\n【64†Full size table】\n\nA series of ACBC tasks were completed by all participants to identify preferences for features and feature levels for encouraging park-based physical activity. An ACBC survey is interactive as tasks are customised in real time based on individual preferences reported as participants progress through the survey [【65†43】]. In the previous rating study features were examined independently or in isolation, whereas with ACBC the features are examined conjointly. ACBC surveys tend to be realistic of people’s real life decision-making as they employ non-compensatory procedures [【66†44】]. Participants were required to complete each task before progressing to the next task. Firstly, respondents selected six features from the list of 13 that would be most likely to encourage them to be active in the park. If any of the six selected features had three or four levels, participants were required to select their most preferred level. For example, for the feature ‘slides’, participants were asked to select their preferred level: ‘giant slides’, ‘medium slides’, or ‘no slides’.\n\nRespondents were then shown a series of six ‘screening’ questions where four parks were described; each park had a different combination of feature levels for the six selected features. For each park, participants were required to indicate if the park described ‘would’ or ‘would not’ encourage them to be active. Based on responses to these screening questions, the program identified if any particular feature levels were included in parks that were selected as ‘not encouraging’ them to be active. In those cases, participants were asked additional question(s) to indicate whether any of the identified feature levels would be ‘totally unacceptable’ and to select the one feature level that was most unacceptable. Similarly, when the program’s algorithm detected that certain feature levels were consistently included, respondents were asked to select the feature level that a park ‘must have’ to encourage them to be active when at the park. These ‘must have’ and ‘unacceptable’ questions determined whether specific feature levels were non-compensatory for choice and ensured that remaining tasks included features levels that best met each individual’s needs.\n\nParticipants were then shown a series of 13 ‘choice tasks’ that included descriptions of two parks. They were asked to select the park that was most appealing for encouraging them to be active. Each park had a different combination of the levels of the six features that had been identified in the previous sections as ‘possibilities’ (i.e., feature levels had not been marked as ‘unacceptable’). The “winning” park profile for each choice task tournament was included in the following rounds of the choice task tournament until the most preferred profile was determined [【66†44】]. Examples of each of the main tasks are included in Additional file 1. Pilot testing was performed with two girls and two boys aged 7-12 years, and minor amendments for clarity were made accordingly.\n\n### Data analyses \n\nDescriptive characteristics of the sample were calculated using Stata version 15 (Stata Corp., College Station, TX, USA). Data from the ACBC survey were analysed using Sawtooth SSI Web Lighthouse Studio 9.8.0. ACBC analysis yield two t",

                "type": "webpage",
                "title": "Urban park design and children’s physical activity levels: an investigation of design characteristics of green areas and playgrounds | Journal of Engineering and Applied Science | Full Text",
                "url": "https://jeas.springeropen.com/articles/10.1186/s44147-022-00152-x",
                "text": "\n### Playgrounds and children’s physical activity \n\nThe availability of a variety of play areas and equipment that allow both structured and unstructured activities, such as climbing, swinging, and games such as tag, and “make believe” activities, adds quality to parks [【61†44】]. Floyd et al. [【27†10】] reported that within parks, the physical activity levels of girls are generally lower than those of boys. However, according to Korca et al. [【62†45】], playgrounds are the only activity setting in parks that attracts both boys and girls equally. Smith [【63†46】] also suggested that boys are more willing to take risks than girls during play, particularly in playgrounds. Previous research shows that the type of playground and the equipment provided influence children’s creativity and physical activity levels [【64†47】, 【65†48】]. According to the literature [【66†49】,【67†50】,【68†51】], playgrounds can be classified into three main types: traditional playgrounds (categorized by the presence of unconnected play equipment such as slides, swings, seesaws, and climbers), contemporary playgrounds (in which the different pieces of play equipment are typically connected together to form multi-purpose continuous structures with multiple entry and exit points and that are usually aesthetically pleasing), and adventure playgrounds (which incorporate various types of loose materials—old tires, large building blocks, ropes, logs and various pieces of wood, buckets and water containers, sand and sand toys, and a variety of natural materials—that allow children to create their own environment or play equipment). Outdoor playgrounds are intended to enhance children’s physical, social, emotional, and cognitive development through play and are typically designed to provide a relatively safe playing environment [【69†52】]. However, several researchers have argued for the developmental benefits of the less predictable and more dynamic play spaces for children over those designed for safety [【70†53】, 【71†54】]. According to Withagen et al. [【72†55】], one of the factors determining the attractiveness of a playground to children is the effort needed to actualize an affordance in that playground, as it has been suggested that children are attracted to challenging opportunities that imply risky behavior [【51†34】].\n\n## Methods\n\n### Research design \n\nAs previously mentioned, the objective of the study is to investigate, in the Egyptian context, the design characteristics of the green areas and playgrounds of urban parks that promote different levels of children’s physical activity. To achieve this objective, the study investigated six settings—three green areas and three playgrounds—in a large urban park east of Cairo, namely “Family Park”. Family Park is a relatively new urban park in New Cairo with a total area of about 283,000 sqm. This park was selected for the study because it features several green areas and several playgrounds. In addition, the different green areas and the different playgrounds differ in terms of design characteristics. The study relied on a mixed qualitative-quantitative approach and several methods of data collection, including the field documentation of the design characteristics of the six settings investigated, structured observations, behavioral mapping, and semi-structured interviews with 16 children. The purpose of the structured observations was to quantitatively assess levels of children’s physical activity in the different settings investigated. Behavioral mapping was used to document the different activities children undertake in relation to the design features of the six settings. Finally, the purpose of the semi-structured interviews was to investigate children’s perceptions, opinions, and preferences about the different settings studied.\n\nBased on the literature discussing the age range for fundamental motor skills development, the target age group for this study was 5- to 11-year-old children [【36†19】,【37†20】,【38†21】]. Children under the age of 5 were not included because it is only from the age of five that children can interpret their experiences and feelings in relation to their environments and can express their preferences about the places they use and the activities they undertake in those places [【73†56】]. Moreover, children under the age of five are often unable to use the playground on their own and are usually accompanied by a parent or guardian. Accordingly, only 5- to 11-year-old children were interviewed. For observations and behavior mapping, the determination of children’s age had to rely on judgment and only children who appeared within the targeted age group were considered.\n\n### The settings selected for the study \n\nSix settings within family park were selected for the study, three green areas and three playgrounds. Family park features several relatively well spatially defined green areas and a total of four playgrounds. Some of the green areas border a large artificial lake at the center of the park. The park also features a petting zoo, an outdoor theater, a small amusement park, and several buildings housing a museum, workshop spaces for children, indoor games, and restaurants. A main circular pathway connects the different areas and facilities of the park together.\n\nThe three green areas selected for the study—GA1, GA2, and GA3 (see Fig. 【74†1】)—were determined during pilot visits to be the green areas most used by children. These three green areas were also selected because, as detailed in Table 【75†1】, they differ in terms of physical characteristics such as area, topography, and types and diversity of vegetation. They also differ in their relation to the park’s central lake. GA1 overlooks the lake. GA2 surrounds the southern end of the lake. Howev",

                "text": "\nclear\n\nfirst_page\n\n【68† Download PDF 】 \n\nsettings\n\n【69† Order Article Reprints 】 \n\nFont Type: \n\nArial Georgia Verdana\n\nFont Size:\n\nAa Aa Aa\n\nLine Spacing:\n\n  \n\nColumn Width:\n\n  \n\nBackground:\n\nOpen Access Article\n\n#  The Impact of a Child-Friendly Design on Children’s Activities in Urban Community Pocket Parks \n\nby \n\nLe Zhang\n\n[Image 0]Le Zhang\n\n【70† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【71† Scilit †scilit.net】【72† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【58† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,2, \n\nXiaoxiao Xu\n\n[Image 1]Xiaoxiao Xu\n\n【73† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【74† Scilit †scilit.net】【75† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【59† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,2 and \n\nYanlong Guo\n\n[Image 2]Yanlong Guo\n\n【76† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【77† Scilit †scilit.net】【78† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【60† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n3,*\n\n^{1}\n\nSchool of Arts, Anhui Jianzhu University, Hefei 230601, China\n\n^{2}\n\nAnhui Provincial Institute of Culture and Tourism Innovation and Development, Hefei 203106, China\n\n^{3}\n\nSocial Innovation Design Research Centre, Anhui University, Hefei 203106, China\n\n*\n\nAuthor to whom correspondence should be addressed. \n\nSustainability 2023, 15(13), 10073; 【79†https://doi.org/10.3390/su151310073†doi.org】 \n\nSubmission received: 11 May 2023 / Revised: 12 June 2023 / Accepted: 23 June 2023 / Published: 25 June 2023\n\n(This article belongs to the Special Issue 【80†Maintaining a Healthy Lifestyle: Sustainability of Promoting Physical Activity】)  \n\nDownload keyboard_arrow_down\n\n 【68†Download PDF】   \nDownload PDF with Cover   \nDownload XML   \n【81†Download Epub】   \n\nBrowse Figures \n\n 【82† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【83† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【84† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【85† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 【86† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 \n\n【87†Review Reports】 【88†Versions Notes】 \n\n## Abstract\n\n:\n\nUrbanization is a global trend that is expected to continue, and by 2025, it is estimated that almost 60% of the world’s children will live in urban areas. Urban community pocket parks provide a solution to the need for parks in high-density urban communities due to their flexible location, small size, and patchy distribution. This paper aims to examine and optimize the construction of urban community pocket parks from a child’s perspective to encourage children’s participation in these parks. The first step was to conduct a literature review to identify key evaluation indicators for assessing the child-friendliness of pocket parks. Then, the AHP-entropy TOPSIS approach was used to establish an indicator system to effectively evaluate the child-friendliness of pocket parks in urban communities. The system included physical space, cognitive ability, emotional development, environmental perception, and social interaction. Finally, suggestions for optimization were made based on the weighting of influencing factors. The results show that freedom of movement (6.2%) significantly affects the child-friendliness ratings of community pocket parks. Additionally, Hefei residents are not sufficiently influenced by the diversity of play (2.29%) and play facility planning (2.58%) in pocket parks. Therefore, consideration should be given to focusing on the degree of nature adaptation in park construction and renewal projects, as well as understanding children’s perception of nature.\n\nKeywords: \n\n【89†child-friendly environment】; 【90†pocket parks】; 【91†evaluation framework】; 【92†AHP】; 【93†entropy weights】; 【94†TOPSIS】\n\n##  1. Introduction \n\nIn June 2019, the China Community Development Association launched the “Code for Building Child-Friendly Communities in China”. According to this code, a neighborhood is defined as a social community of people residing within a certain geographical area [【95†1】]. As globalization continues to increase and cities undergo modernization, urban population growth has surged significantly. The United Nations projects that by 2050, 68% of the global population will be concentrated in urban centers. With this pace of urbanization, it is expected that, by 2025, urban areas will be home to around 60% of the world’s children. However, there is a significant tension between children and cities due to the growing size of cities and the increasing demands for quality of life. Therefore, promoting child-friendly cities is essential for providing good quality of life and accessibility for all children in urban areas. This is especially important in public spaces such as pocket parks, where children are most active. Child-friendly design in such spaces can create a safe, enjoyable, and stimulating environment that promotes sustainable physical activity for children [【96†2】].\n\nA pocket park is defined as a park that is smaller than most urban parks [【97†3】], and the definition of pocket park size varies from region to region, from less than half an acre to less than 5000 square meters [【98†4】]. American scholar Robert Zane designed the world’s first pocket park in 1963, marking the birth of the micropark out of which the prototype was a small park scattered in high-density urban cen",

                "text": "\nArial Georgia Verdana\n\nFont Size:\n\nAa Aa Aa\n\nLine Spacing:\n\n  \n\nColumn Width:\n\n  \n\nBackground:\n\nOpen Access Review\n\n#  Determining Factors in the Use of Urban Parks That Influence the Practice of Physical Activity in Children: A Systematic Review \n\nby \n\nRosario Padial-Ruz\n\n[Image 0]Rosario Padial-Ruz\n\n【73† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【74† Scilit †scilit.net】【75† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【59† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1, \n\nMª Esther Puga-González\n\n[Image 1]Mª Esther Puga-González\n\n【76† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【77† Scilit †scilit.net】【78† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【60† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n1,*, \n\nÁlvaro Céspedes-Jiménez\n\n[Image 2]Álvaro Céspedes-Jiménez\n\n【79† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【80† Scilit †scilit.net】【81† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【61† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n2 and \n\nDavid Cabello-Manrique\n\n[Image 3]David Cabello-Manrique\n\n【82† SciProfiles †sciprofiles.com】【83† Scilit †scilit.net】【84† Preprints.org †www.preprints.org】【62† Google Scholar †scholar.google.com】\n\n2\n\n^{1}\n\nDepartment of Didactics of Musical, Plastic and Corporal, University of Granada, 18011 Granada, Spain\n\n^{2}\n\nDepartment of Physical Education and Sports, University of Granada, 18011 Granada, Spain\n\n*\n\nAuthor to whom correspondence should be addressed. \n\nInt. J. Environ. Res. Public Health 2021, 18(7), 3648; 【85†https://doi.org/10.3390/ijerph18073648†doi.org】 \n\nSubmission received: 22 February 2021 / Revised: 19 March 2021 / Accepted: 24 March 2021 / Published: 31 March 2021\n\n(This article belongs to the Special Issue 【86†Physical Activity in Childhood and Adolescence】)  \n\nDownload keyboard_arrow_down\n\n 【71†Download PDF】   \nDownload PDF with Cover   \nDownload XML   \n【87†Download Epub】   \n\nBrowse Figure \n\n 【88† †pub.mdpi-res.com】 \n\n【89†Versions Notes】 \n\n## Abstract\n\n:\n\nThe design and/or remodelling of urban parks is a good health strategy to alleviate the lack of physical activity (PA) in children and, consequently, the different health problems derived from this. The main objective of the present study was to obtain a systematic review of the design features and characteristics that influence users’ visits to urban parks and the PA engagement in them. A literature search was carried out in the Web of Science (WOS) and Scopus databases during the months of June and July 2020. After considering and applying inclusion criteria, the final review sample was formed of 31 scientific papers published between 2010–2020. The results obtained in the review lead us to conclude that the needs of the population (children and family members who care for them) and socio-economic context of the area in which they are built must be considered when constructing and/or remodelling parks. Involving community members in playground renovations can have a positive effect on park use and PA engagement in children.\n\nKeywords: \n\n【90†urban parks】; 【91†children】; 【92†user perception】; 【93†design】; 【94†physical activity】; 【95†health】\n\n##  1. Introduction \n\nThe high rates of inactivity and sedentariness amongst children worldwide is a major problem that needs to be addressed immediately [【96†1】]. World Health Organisation (WHO) recommendations for physical activity (PA) in young people, aged 5–17 years, state at least 60 min per day of moderate to vigorous intensity activity. This activity should be mostly aerobic and should incorporate vigorous activities that particularly strengthen muscles and bones at least three times per week. However, over the past few years, we have seen an increase in the proportion of the population that does not comply with these recommendations. This includes a large number of children [【97†2】,【98†3】], with more children also coming from disadvantaged settings [【99†4】].\n\nInactivity at early ages can lead to high obesity rates. Excess body fat in the early stages of life can have serious health consequences at later stages, since the risk of contracting diseases derived from inactivity, such as diabetes, hypertension and coronary diseases, among others, is high and dangerous [【100†5】]. Thus, according to Omorou et al. [【101†6】], lack of PA may increase the likelihood of being obese in adulthood.\n\nAnother consequence of childhood obesity comes from the association between motor skills, PA and body composition in the pre-school stage. Diminished interaction with natural environments and the restriction of movement at increasingly younger ages can lead to poor execution of the motor tasks necessary for correct psychomotor development [【102†7】].\n\nAt a motor level, children will also present greater difficulties at a later stage when doing sports. Children with higher physical activity levels and less sedentary behaviour present better motor skills compared with those who mostly engage in sedentary pursuits, with the latter often unable to develop such skills adequately [【103†8】]. Nonetheless, inactivity not only causes problems at a physical and physiological level, but it also plays a fundamental role with regards to",
